Complementation of Emerson-Lei Automata (Technical Report)
Vojtěch Havlena, Ondřej Lengál, Barbora Šmahlíková

Abstract
We give new constructions for complementing subclasses of Emerson-Lei automata using modifications of rank-based Büchi automata complementation. In particular, we propose a specialized rank-based construction for a Boolean combination of Inf acceptance conditions, which heavily relies on a novel way of a run DAG labelling enhancing the ranking functions with models of the acceptance condition. Moreover, we propose a technique for complementing generalized Rabin automata, which are structurally as concise as general Emerson-Lei automata (but can have a larger acceptance condition). The construction is modular in the sense that it combines a given complementation algorithm for a condition φ \varphi in a way that the resulting procedure handles conditions of the form Fin ∧ φ \textrm{Fin}\land\varphi . The proposed constructions give upper bounds that are exponentially better than the state of the art for some of the classes.



1
Introduction

Complementation of
\omega
-automata is an important operation in formal
verification with various applications, for example in model checking wrt
expressive temporal logics such as QPTL
[
23
]
or
HyperLTL
[
10
]
; testing language inclusion of

\omega
-automata, or in decision procedures of various
logics
[
6
,
19
]
.
For Büchi automata (BAs)—i.e.,

\omega
-automata with the simplest acceptance condition—complementation has
been, from the theoretical point of view, thoroughly explored,
starting with constructions having the
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(n)}}
state
complexity
[
6
]
coming down to constructions asymptotically
matching the lower bound
\Omega((0.76n)^{n})
(modulo a polynomial
factor)
[
36
,
1
]
.
Over the years,
\omega
-automata with more complex acceptance conditions (such
as generalized Büchi (GBAs), (generalized) Rabin/Streett, parity) have found
uses in practice.
The most general acceptance condition used is the so-called
Emerson-Lei

condition
[
11
]
, which is
an arbitrary Boolean formula consisting of
\mathsf{Fin}
and
\mathsf{Inf}
atoms.

\mathsf{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}

denotes that all transitions labeled with
c

must occur only finitely often in an accepting run and
\mathsf{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})

denotes that there must be a transition labeled with
c
occurring
infinitely often.
There are two main reasons for using more complex acceptance conditions:

(i)

more compact representation of automata and

(ii)

the ability to determinize
(deterministic BAs are strictly less
expressive than BAs).

From the theoretical point of view, precise bounds on complementation of
automata with more complex acceptance condition is much less researched,
demonstrated by the best upper bound for (transition-based) Emerson-Lei
automata (TELAs) being
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(n)}}

[
35
]
states.
Here, the
\mathcal{O}
in the exponent can hide a linear (or constant) factor, which
would have a doubly-exponential effect, giving little information about the
actual complexity.
In this paper, we present complementation algorithms for several subclasses of
TELAs and thoroughly study their complexity, giving better upper bounds than
the currently-best known algorithms.

Our contributions can be summarized as follows:

1.

We propose a rank-based complementation algorithm for
Inf
-TELAs,
i.e., TELAs where the acceptance condition does not contain any
Fin

atom, with the state complexity
\mathcal{O}(n(0.76nk)^{n})
where
n
is the
number of states and
k
is the number of
minimal models
of the
acceptance condition.

2.

By instantiating the previously mentioned algorithm, we obtain
a complementation algorithm for generalized Büchi automata with
k

colours constructing a BA with the state complexity

\mathcal{O}(n(0.76nk)^{n})
, which is, to the best of our knowledge, better than the best previously known
algorithms.

3.

We propose a modular procedure for complementing TELAs with the
acceptance condition
\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\land\varphi
given
a compatible complementation procedure for formula
\varphi
.

upper bound
\mathcal{O}(n^{2^{k}}(0.76nk)^{n2^{k}})
, which is the best upper bound
for complementation of general TELAs that we are aware of.

2
Preliminaries

We fix a finite non-empty alphabet
\Sigma
and the first infinite
ordinal
\omega
.
For
k\in\omega
, we use
\lfloor\!\!\lfloor k\rfloor\!\!\rfloor
to represent the largest even number less than or equal
to
k
, e.g.,
\lfloor\!\!\lfloor 43\rfloor\!\!\rfloor=\lfloor\!\!\lfloor 42\rfloor\!\!\rfloor=42
.
An (infinite) word
w
is a function
w\colon\omega\to\Sigma
where
the
i
-th symbol is denoted as
w_{i}
.
Sometimes, we represent
w
as an infinite sequence
w=w_{0}w_{1}\dots

We denote the set of all infinite words over
\Sigma
as
\Sigma^{\omega}
;
an
\omega
-language
is a subset of
\Sigma^{\omega}
.
We use
\cdot
for ellipsis, e.g., if interested only in the second component

of a triple, we may write the triple as
(\cdot,x,\cdot)
.

2.1
Emerson-Lei Acceptance Conditions

Given a set
\Gamma=\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
of
k

colours
(often depicted as
0
,
1
, etc.), we define the
set of
Emerson-Lei acceptance conditions

\mathbb{EL}(\Gamma)
as
the set of formulae constructed according to the following grammar:

\displaystyle\alpha::=\mathit{tt}\mid\mathit{ff}\mid\mathsf{Inf}(c)\mid\mathsf{Fin}(c)\mid(\alpha\land\alpha)\mid(\alpha\lor\alpha)

for
c\in\Gamma
.
The
satisfaction
relation
\models
for a set of colours
M\subseteq\Gamma
and a condition
\alpha
is defined inductively as follows (for
c\in\Gamma
):

\displaystyle M\models\mathit{tt},

\displaystyle M\models\mathsf{Fin}(c)

\displaystyle\text{~ iff ~}c\notin M,

\displaystyle M\models\alpha_{1}\lor\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\text{~ iff ~}M\models\alpha_{1}\text{ or }M\models\alpha_{2},

\displaystyle M\not\models\mathit{ff},

\displaystyle M\models\mathsf{Inf}(c)

\displaystyle\text{~ iff ~}c\in M,

\displaystyle M\models\alpha_{1}\land\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle\text{~ iff ~}M\models\alpha_{1}\text{ and }M\models\alpha_{2}.

If
M\models\alpha
, we say that
M
is a
model
of
\alpha

We denote by
|\alpha|
the number of atomic conditions contained in
\alpha
,
where multiple occurrences of the same atomic condition are counted multiple
times.

\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{ex}}

A (nondeterministic) transition-based
1
1

1

We only consider transition-based acceptance in order to avoid cluttering the
paper by dealing with accepting states
and
accepting transitions.
Extending our approach to state/transition-based (or just state-based) automata
is straightforward.

Emerson-Lei automaton
(TELA)
over
\Sigma
is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{Q},\delta,I,\Gamma,\mathsf{p},\mathsf{Acc})
,
where
\mathcal{Q}
is a finite set of
states
(we often use
n
to denote
|\mathcal{Q}|
),

\delta\subseteq\mathcal{Q}\times\Sigma\times\mathcal{Q}
is a set of

transitions
2
2

2

Note that there is also a more general definition
of TELAs with
\delta\subseteq\mathcal{Q}\times\Sigma\times 2^{\Gamma}\times\mathcal{Q}
; in this paper, we use the simpler one.
,

I\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
is the set of
initial
states,

\Gamma
is the set of
colours
,

\mathsf{p}\colon\delta\to 2^{\Gamma}
is a
colouring
of transitions, and

\mathsf{Acc}\in\mathbb{EL}(\Gamma)
.
We use
p\overset{a}{\rightarrow}q
to denote that
(p,a,q)\in\delta
and sometimes
treat
\delta
as a function
\delta\colon\mathcal{Q}\times\Sigma\to 2^{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Moreover, we extend
\delta
to sets of states
P\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
as

\delta(P,a)=\bigcup_{p\in P}\delta(p,a)
.
See
Fig.

1
for an example TELA

over
\Sigma=\{a,b,c\}
with 3 colours

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
.
We define
|\mathcal{A}|=|\mathcal{Q}|
.

A
run

of
\mathcal{A}
from
q\in\mathcal{Q}
on an input word
w
is an infinite sequence
\rho\colon\omega\to\mathcal{Q}
that starts in
q
and respects
\delta
, i.e.,
\rho(0)=q
and

\forall i\geq 0\colon\rho(i)\overset{w_{i}}{\rightarrow}\rho(i+1)\in\delta
.
Let
\mathit{inf}(\rho)\subseteq\delta
denote the set of
transitions occurring in
\rho
infinitely often and
\mathit{inf}_{\!\!\Gamma}(\rho)=\bigcup\{\mathsf{p}(x)\mid x\in\mathit{inf}(\rho)\}
be the set of infinitely
often occurring colours.
A run
\rho
is
accepting
wrt an acceptance condition
\alpha
, written
as
\rho\models\alpha
, iff
\mathit{inf}_{\!\!\Gamma}(\rho)\models\alpha
and

\rho
is accepting in
\mathcal{A}
iff
\rho\models\mathsf{Acc}
.
The
language
of
\mathcal{A}
, denoted as
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, is defined as the set of words

w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
for which there exists an accepting run in
\mathcal{A}

starting with some state in
I
.
Classical acceptance conditions can be in this more general framework described
as follows (we only provide those used later in the paper and include their abbreviations):

2.3
Run DAGs

In this section, we recall the terminology from
[
18
]
(which is
a minor modification of the terminology from
[
25
]
and

[
36
]
) used heavily in the paper.
Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{Q},\delta,I,\Gamma,\mathsf{p},\mathsf{Acc})
be
a TELA.
We fix the definition of the
run DAG
of
\mathcal{A}
over a word
w
to be
a DAG (directed acyclic graph)
\mathcal{G}_{w}=(V,E)
of vertices
V
and edges
E

where

•

V\subseteq\mathcal{Q}\times\omega
s.t.
(q,i)\in V
iff there is
a run
\rho
of
\mathcal{A}
from
I
over
w
with
\rho_{i}=q
,

•

E\subseteq V\times V
s.t.
((q,i),(q^{\prime},i^{\prime}))\in E
iff
i^{\prime}=i+1

and
q^{\prime}\in\delta(q,w_{i})
.

q,0
q,1
r,1
q,2
t,2
s,2
q,3
s,3
q,4
r,4
q,5
t,5
s,5
q,6
\vdots
\ddots
\vdots
0
1
0
0
c
a
a
c
a
b
c
\vdots

rank 2

Figure 2
:
A labelled run DAG of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{ex}}
over the word

caa(cab)^{\omega}\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{ex}})

See
Fig.

2
for an example of a run DAG of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{ex}}
from

Fig.

1
over the word
caa(cab)^{\omega}\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{ex}})

(we will return to the additional labels in the figure later).
Given a DAG
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
,
we often identify
\mathcal{G}
with
V
, for instance, we will write
(p,i)\in\mathcal{G}
to denote that
(p,i)\in V
.
For a vertex
v\in\mathcal{G}
, we denote the set of vertices of
\mathcal{G}
reachable
from
v
(including
v
itself) as
\mathit{reach}_{\mathcal{G}}(v)
or just
\mathit{reach}(v)

if
\mathcal{G}
is clear from the context.
A vertex
v\in\mathcal{G}
is
finite
iff
\mathit{reach}(v)
is finite and

infinite
if it is not finite.
In
Fig.

2
, the vertices
(s,2),(s,3),(s,5),\ldots
are
finite and all other vertices are infinite.
Moreover, for a colour
\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}

-endangered.
A pair of vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
is
converging
iff
\mathit{reach}(v_{1})\cap\mathit{reach}(v_{2})\neq\emptyset
(
v_{1}
and
v_{2}

converge
).
A function
r\colon V\to\omega
is a
run DAG ranking
if for every
v\in V
it holds that
\forall u\in\mathit{reach}(v)\colon r(u)\leq r(v)
.
We use
\max(r)
to denote the
rank
of
r
, i.e., the maximum value from

\{r(u)\mid u\in V\}
if it exists and
\infty
otherwise.
A ranking
r
of
\mathcal{G}
is called
tight
iff
there exists a level
i
such that

(i)

m=\max\{r((q,i))\mid q\in\mathcal{Q}\}
is odd and

(ii)

for all levels
j\geq i
it holds that
\{1,3,\dots,m\}\subseteq\{r((q,j))\mid q\in\mathcal{Q}\}
.

3
Complementation of
Inf
-TELAs

In this section, we describe a complement construction for
Inf
-TELAs.
Our approach is an extension of rank-based BA
complementation
algorithms
[
25
,
13
,
36
,
22
,
9
,
15
,
18
]
,
which construct a BA whose runs simulate a run DAG ranking procedure.
We start with giving the run DAG ranking procedure (which extends the ranking
procedure from
[
25
]
with the introduction of models) and then
proceed to the complementation algorithm itself.
One can see our algorithm also as an improvement of the algorithm for
complementing GBAs in
[
26
]
by

(i)

introducing model assignments,

(ii)

getting better complexity through the use of tight rankings, and

(iii)

generalizing the construction from GBAs to arbitrary
Inf
-TELAs.

3.1
Inf
-TELA Run DAG Labelling

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{Q},\delta,I,\Gamma,\mathsf{p},\mathsf{Acc})
be an
Inf
-TELA.
We use
\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}
to denote the propositional formula obtained
from
\mathsf{Acc}
by replacing conjunctions by disjunctions and vice versa, and
substituting atoms of the form

Furthermore, we use
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
to denote the set of
minimal
models
of
\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
is the set where

(i)

for every model
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
, there exists a model
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
such that
m^{\prime}\subseteq m
, and

(ii)

there are no
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
such that
m\subset m^{\prime}
.

We note that
\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
can be obtained as the upward closure
of
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
(and
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
is an
antichain
).

.
Moreover, we use
\textbf{lex-min}(\overline{\mathsf{Acc}})
to denote the lexicographically
smallest model from
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
(w.l.o.g., we assume

\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}\neq\emptyset
).

\textbf{lex-min}(\overline{\mathsf{Acc}})
is used to pinpoint one model when any model can
be used.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V,E)
be a run DAG of
\mathcal{A}
over
w
.
For a set of vertices

U\subseteq V
, a mapping
\eta\colon U\to\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
is called

endangered in
\mathcal{G}
if

1.

\eta
is finite and nonempty,

2.

each
v\in U
is
\eta(v)
-endangered in
\mathcal{G}
, and

3.

for each pair of vertices
v_{1},v_{2}\in U
converging in
\mathcal{G}
, we
have
\eta(v_{1})=\eta(v_{2})
.

A function
m
with the signature
m\colon V\to\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
is called a
model
assignment
.
For instance, for
\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{ex}}
in
Fig.

1
, we have

Algorithm 1

Inf
-TELA run DAG labelling

Input:

A run DAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}
of
\mathcal{A}
over
w
, acceptance condition
\mathsf{Acc}

Output:

A run DAG ranking
r
and a model assignment
m
if
w\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, else
\bot

1

i\leftarrow 0
,
r\leftarrow\emptyset
,
m\leftarrow\emptyset
;
//
i\in\omega
,
r\colon V\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\{0,\ldots,2|\mathcal{Q}|\}
,
m\colon V\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}

2

\mathcal{G}^{0}=(V^{0},E^{0})\leftarrow\mathcal{G}_{w}
without finite
vertices;

3

foreach

v\in\mathcal{G}_{w}
s.t.
v
is finite

do

4

r(v)\leftarrow 0
,
m(v)\leftarrow\textbf{lex-min}(\overline{\mathsf{Acc}})
;

5

while

\mathcal{G}^{i}\neq\emptyset

do

6

if

\exists(\eta\colon U\to\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}})
s.t.
U\subseteq V^{i}
and
\eta
is endangered in
\mathcal{G}^{i}

then

7

foreach

v\in U
and
u\in\mathit{reach}_{\mathcal{G}^{i}}(v)

do

8

r(u)\leftarrow i+1
,
m(u)\leftarrow\eta(v)
;

9

\mathcal{G}^{i+1}\leftarrow\mathcal{G}^{i}
without vertices with the rank
i+1
;

10

foreach

v\in\mathcal{G}^{i+1}
s.t.
v
is finite in
\mathcal{G}^{i+1}

do

11

r(v)\leftarrow i+2
,
m(v)\leftarrow\textbf{lex-min}(\overline{\mathsf{Acc}})
;

12

\mathcal{G}^{i+2}\leftarrow\mathcal{G}^{i+1}
without vertices with the rank
i+2
;

13

i\leftarrow i+2
;

14

else

15

return

\bot
;

16

return

(r,m)
;

In
Algorithm

1
, we give a (nondeterministic) ranking procedure that
assigns ranks and minimal models of
\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}
to each
vertex of
\mathcal{G}
.
Intuitively, the algorithm starts by giving all initially finite vertices the
rank 0 and assigning their model to
\textbf{lex-min}(\overline{\mathsf{Acc}})
(Line
1
).
Then, it proceeds in iterations, each starting with the DAG
\mathcal{G}^{i}
and
consisting of two steps:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
conditions, i.e.,
\eta
is endangered).
If such a model assignment exists, the algorithm assigns rank
i+1
to all
vertices reachable from
U
and removes them from the DAG, creating
DAG
\mathcal{G}^{i+1}
(Lines
1
–
1
).

2.

Second, the algorithm assigns rank
i+2
to all vertices
in
\mathcal{G}^{i+1}
that became finite (after the previous step) and removes
them from the DAG, creating DAG
\mathcal{G}^{i+2}
(Lines
1
–
1
).
The counter
i
is incremented by two and the next iteration continues.

The iterations end when
\mathcal{G}^{i}
is empty or when no suitable model
assignment
\eta
is found (which happens when
w
is accepted by
\mathcal{A}
).
Note that due to the nondeterminism within the algorithm, it may be possible to
obtain, in two different runs of the algorithm on the same run DAG, two
different pairs
(r_{1},m_{1})
and
(r_{2},m_{2})
with
\max(r_{1})\neq\max(r_{2})
.

\mathcal{A}_{\mathit{ex}}

Lemma 1

If
Algorithm

1
returns
\bot
, then
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}=\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}^{\prime}}}
contains sets of colours
M\subseteq\Gamma
, each of them with at
least one colour from
\varphi_{1}
, at least one colour from
\varphi_{2}
, etc.
In order for
Algorithm

1
to return
\bot
, it needs to hold that
there is some
i\geq 0
such that
\mathcal{G}^{i}
is nonempty and there does not
exist any mapping
\eta\colon U\to\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
, with
U\subseteq V^{i}
, that
would be endangered in
\mathcal{G}^{i}
.
In particular, such a (nonempty) mapping
\eta
does not exist iff no vertex

v\in\mathcal{G}^{i}
satisfies point (
2
) of the
definition of an endangered mapping (i.e., when we can find an accepting path
from all vertices remaining in
\mathcal{G}^{i}
).
Therefore, it follows that no vertex
v\in\mathcal{G}^{i}
is
M
-endangered for any
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
, i.e., in other words,

for every vertex
v\in\mathcal{G}^{i}
there is some clause
\varphi_{j}
such that
v

can in
\mathcal{G}^{i}
reach a
c^{j}_{p}
-edge for each
1\leq p\leq k_{j}
.

r,1

s,3

.

t_{i}

from

, we set the new value to
c^{j}_{1}
.
The path
\pi
is then constructed as an infinite path that goes through the
infinite sequence
v_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\ldots

Note that because the sequence
v_{0},e_{1},e_{2}\ldots
is infinite, due to the
pigeonhole principle there will be a clause
\varphi_{j}
s.t. the sequence

t_{0},t_{1},\ldots
infinitely often increments the
j
-th component and
so
\pi
is accepting.
From
\pi
, we can now extract the accepting run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
.
∎

Lemma 2

Algorithm

1
always terminates with
i\leq 2n
.

Proof

Consider a run DAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}
for a word
w
. First observe that at the end of
the main loop of
Algorithm

1
(Line
1
),

\mathcal{G}^{i}
has no finite vertices (all of them were removed).
Due to Line
1
,
\mathcal{G}^{i}
at
the beginning of the main loop (Line
1
) also has no finite vertices.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{i}
be
the DAG
(V^{i}_{m},E^{i}\cap(V^{i}_{m}\times V^{i}_{m}))
where
V^{i}_{m}=\{(q,j)\in V^{i}\mid j\geq m\}
, i.e., the projection of
\mathcal{G}^{i}
from level
m
down,
and
0pt(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{i})
is the maximum number of vertices on any level of the
run DAG below level
m
, formally,
0pt(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{i})=\max\{|\{(q,j):(q,j)\in V^{i}_{m}\}|:j\geq m\}
.
From the definition of endangered mapping and the loop
on Line
1
, we have that if the condition on
Line
1
holds, there is some
m\in\omega
s.t.
0pt(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{i+1})<0pt(\mathcal{G}_{m}^{i})
.
This holds because if the mapping
\eta
is non-empty, then there is at least
one infinite path
\mathcal{G}^{i}
that is all removed in the next step, i.e., from
some level
m
, the width of all levels below get decreased by at least one.
If the condition on Line
1
does not hold, the algorithm
terminates and we are done.
From the previous claim we have that in each successful iteration of the main
loop, the width of
\mathcal{G}^{i+2}
in the limit is at most the one of

\mathcal{G}^{i}
minus one.
Since the maximum width of
\mathcal{G}_{w}
is
n
, then, if
w\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, at latest
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{2n-1}
is empty for some
m\in\omega
,
and hence
\mathcal{G}^{2n}
is empty and the algorithm terminates.
∎

Lemma 3

If
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, then
Algorithm

1
terminates with
\bot
.

Proof

Consider some
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then, there is an accepting run
\rho
on
w
in
\mathcal{A}
.
We have
(\rho_{j},j)\in\mathcal{G}_{w}
for all
j\in\omega
; we show
that
(\rho_{j},j)
is not
M
-endangered for every
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
.
The fact that no
\rho_{j}
is finite follows from the fact that
\rho
is
infinite.
Observe that for each
M\in\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
, there is some

s.t.

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in\textrm{Inf}(\rho)
(otherwise,
w
would not be
accepted by
\mathcal{A}
).
Therefore,
(\rho_{j},j)
is not
M
-endangered.

Hence, in every iteration of
Algorithm

1
, all
vertices
(\rho_{j},j)
stay in
\mathcal{G}^{i}
.
From
Lemma

2
we have that
Algorithm

1

always terminates, but
\mathcal{G}^{i}\neq\emptyset
for each
i
.
Therefore, the algorithm terminates with
\bot
.
∎

Corollary 1

w\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
iff
Algorithm

1
on

\mathcal{G}_{w}
terminates with
(r,m)
.

Proof

(\Rightarrow)
follows from
Lemma

1
by contraposition and

(\Leftarrow)
follows from
Lemma

3
by contraposition.
∎

The following lemma about the ranking procedure will be useful later.

Lemma 4

If
Algorithm

1
terminates with
(r,m)
, then
\max(r)\leq 2n

and, moreover, either
\max(r)=0
or
r
is tight.

Proof

The first part (
\max(r)\leq 2n
) follows directly from
Lemma

2
.
For the second part, there are two options: either
\mathcal{G}_{w}
is finite (i.e., there is no
infinite run of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
), in which case
Algorithm

1

assigns all vertices in
\mathcal{G}_{w}
rank
0
and does not even enter the loop at
Line
1
.
In the other case (
\mathcal{G}
is infinite), let
k=\max(r)
if
\max(r)
is odd
and
k=\max(r)-1
otherwise (from the previous case, we know that
k\geq 1
).
We know that for every
\ell\in\{1,3,\ldots,k\}
, there is a vertex

v_{\ell}=(q_{\ell},i_{\ell})\in\mathcal{G}_{w}
with
r(v_{\ell})=\ell
(this is because
the mapping at Line
1
in the algorithm needs to be non-empty)
and that such a vertex is the beginning of an infinite path of vertices with
rank
\ell
.
Therefore, there needs to be a level
i
containing vertices with all
ranks
\{1,3,\ldots,k\}
.
From the previous, all levels
j>i
will also have all of the odd ranks up
to
k
.
Choosing
i
large enough will prevent level
i
having a vertex with an even rank higher than
k
.
Therefore,
r
is tight.
∎

3.2
Inf
-TELA Complement Construction

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{Q},\delta,I,\Gamma,\mathsf{p},\mathsf{Acc})
be an

Inf
-TELA and
n=|\mathcal{Q}|
.
We define a
(level) ranking
to be a function

f\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{0,\ldots,2n\}
.
The
rank
of
f
is defined as
f=\max\{f(q)\mid q\in\mathcal{Q}\}
.
We call a mapping
\mu\colon Q\to\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
a
level model
.
We say that
\mu
is
consistent
wrt
f
if

(i)

\mu(q)\in\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
if
f(q)
is odd, and

(ii)

\mu(q)=\textbf{lex-min}(\overline{\mathsf{Acc}})
if
f(q)
is even.

We denote the set of all level models by
\mathsf{LM}
.
For a set of states
S\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
and a level model
\mu
, we call
f
to be
(S,\mu)\textrm{-}\mathit{tight}
if

(i) it has an odd rank
r
,

(ii)
f(S)\supseteq\{1,3,\ldots,r\}
,

(iii)
f(\mathcal{Q}\setminus S)=\{0\}
, and

(iv)
\mu
is consistent wrt
f
.

A ranking is
\mu\textrm{-}\mathit{tight}
if it is
(\mathcal{Q},\mu)
-tight; we use
\mathcal{T}
to denote
the set of all
\mu
-tight rankings for some level model
\mu
.

For two level rankings
f,f^{\prime}
and two level models
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
, we say
that
(f^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a
consistent successor
of
(f,\mu)
over
a
,
denoted as
(f,\mu)\mathrel{\hbox to11.3pt{\vbox to4.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-2.1375pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@fillstroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.29993pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.33 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.78 0 L 1.34 2.03 L 2.92 0 L 1.34 -2.03 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.81 0 M 7.9 0 C 7.9 1.15 6.96 2.09 5.81 2.09 C 4.66 2.09 3.73 1.15 3.73 0 C 3.73 -1.15 4.66 -2.09 5.81 -2.09 C 6.96 -2.09 7.9 -1.15 7.9 0 Z M 5.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\delta}^{a}}(f^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
, iff

(i)

\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are consistent wrt
f
and
f^{\prime}
, respectively, and

(ii)

for all
q\in\text{dom}(f)
and
q^{\prime}\in\delta(q,a)
the following holds:

(a)

f^{\prime}(q^{\prime})\leq f(q)
,

(b)

(\mathsf{p}(q\overset{a}{\rightarrow}q^{\prime})\cap\mu(q)\neq\emptyset)\Rightarrow f^{\prime}(q^{\prime})\leq\lfloor\!\!\lfloor f(q)\rfloor\!\!\rfloor
, and

(c)

\mu^{\prime}(q^{\prime})\neq\mu(q)\Rightarrow f^{\prime}(q^{\prime})\leq\lfloor\!\!\lfloor f(q)\rfloor\!\!\rfloor
.

Intuitively, the rankings guess the ranks of states in the run DAG and the level models guess the models assigned to states in the labelling procedure described in Section
3.1
.
Consistent successors respect the labelling procedure. On every path in a run DAG, the ranks are nonincreasing.
If some vertex
v
with an odd rank has an outgoing
c
-edge to
v^{\prime}

and
c
is in the model assigned to
v
, the vertex
v^{\prime}
has to have
a lower rank than
v
, because when
v
is removed from
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{i}
, there is no
reachable
c
-edge in
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{i}
.
Moreover, if the model is changed between
v
and
v^{\prime}
, then the rank also has to be decreased.

The complement of
\mathcal{A}
is given as the BA

\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})=(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},I^{\prime},F^{\prime})
whose components are defined as
follows:

•

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Q}_{1}\cup\mathcal{Q}_{2}
where

–

\mathcal{Q}_{1}=2^{\mathcal{Q}}
and

–

\mathcal{Q}_{2}=\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[t]{ll}\{(S,O,f,i,\mu)\in&2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times\mathcal{T}\times\{0,2,\ldots,2n-2\}\times\mathsf{LM}\mid\\
&f\text{ is }(S,\mu)\textrm{-tight},O\subseteq S\cap f^{-1}(i)\},\end{array}

•

I^{\prime}=\{I\}
,

•

\delta^{\prime}=\delta_{1}\cup\delta_{2}\cup\delta_{3}
where

–

\delta_{1}\colon\mathcal{Q}_{1}\times\Sigma\to 2^{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}
such that
\delta_{1}(S,a)=\{\delta(S,a)\}
,

–

\delta_{2}\colon\mathcal{Q}_{1}\times\Sigma\to 2^{\mathcal{Q}_{2}}
s.t.
\delta_{2}(S,a)=\{(S^{\prime},\emptyset,f,0,\mu)\mid S^{\prime}=\delta(S,a)\}
, and

–

\delta_{3}\colon\mathcal{Q}_{2}\times\Sigma\to 2^{\mathcal{Q}_{2}}
such that
(S^{\prime},O^{\prime},f^{\prime},i^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\delta_{3}((S,O,f,i,\mu),a)
iff

*

S^{\prime}=\delta(S,a)
,

*

(f,\mu)\mathrel{\hbox to11.3pt{\vbox to4.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-2.1375pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.29993pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.33 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.78 0 L 1.34 2.03 L 2.92 0 L 1.34 -2.03 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.81 0 M 7.9 0 C 7.9 1.15 6.96 2.09 5.81 2.09 C 4.66 2.09 3.73 1.15 3.73 0 C 3.73 -1.15 4.66 -2.09 5.81 -2.09 C 6.96 -2.09 7.9 -1.15 7.9 0 Z M 5.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\delta}^{a}}(f^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
,

*

\mathit{rank}(f)=\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})
,

*

and

·

i^{\prime}=(i+2)\mod(\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})+1)
and
O^{\prime}=f^{\prime-1}(i^{\prime})
if

O=\emptyset
or

·

i^{\prime}=i
and
O^{\prime}=\delta(O,a)\cap f^{\prime-1}(i)
if
O\neq\emptyset
, and

•

F^{\prime}=\{\emptyset\overset{a}{\rightarrow}\emptyset\in\delta_{1}\mid a\in\Sigma\}\cup\{M_{1}\overset{a}{\rightarrow}M_{2}\in\delta_{3}\mid M_{1}=(\cdot,\emptyset,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot),a\in\Sigma\}

Intuitively, a run of
\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})
on a word
w
tries to construct
the run DAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}
of
\mathcal{A}
on the same word, with rankings encoded within
the states.
The restrictions on
\delta_{3}
encode the rules from
Algorithm

1
.
The partitioning of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
into
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
allows us to
consider only tight rankings, as in
[
13
]
.
Moreover, the
i
-component of a macrostate allows us further decrease the
number of states in the same way as in
[
36
]
(we know that all states
in
O
have the same rank
i
).

Theorem 3.1

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
Inf
-TELA. Then,
\mathcal{L}(\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A}))=\Sigma^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof

(
\subseteq
)
We use Boolean laws and prove an equivalent statement
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{L}(\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A}))
.
Let
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
be a word and
\rho
be an accepting run
of
\mathcal{A}
on
w
.
First, let
\rho^{\prime}
be the run
\rho^{\prime}=S_{0}S_{1}\ldots
with
S_{0}=I
and

S_{i+1}=\delta_{1}(S_{i},w(i))
for all
i\in\omega
(i.e.,
\rho^{\prime}

stays in
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
).
The run
\rho^{\prime}
cannot be accepting in
\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})
,
because
\rho(i)\in S_{i}
and so
S_{i}\neq\emptyset
for any
i\in\omega

(in
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, the only accepting transitions are those from
state
\emptyset
to state
\emptyset
).
Second, let

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime\prime}=S_{0}S_{1}\ldots S_{p}(S_{p+1},O_{p+1},f_{p+1},i_{p+1},\mu_{p+1})(S_{p+2},O_{p+2},f_{p+2},i_{p+2},\mu_{p+2})\ldots

be a run of
\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})
on
w
(
\rho^{\prime\prime}
jumps to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
at
position
p
).
From the construction, it holds that
(f_{j},\mu_{j})\mathrel{\hbox to11.3pt{\vbox to4.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-2.1375pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.29993pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.33 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.78 0 L 1.34 2.03 L 2.92 0 L 1.34 -2.03 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.81 0 M 7.9 0 C 7.9 1.15 6.96 2.09 5.81 2.09 C 4.66 2.09 3.73 1.15 3.73 0 C 3.73 -1.15 4.66 -2.09 5.81 -2.09 C 6.96 -2.09 7.9 -1.15 7.9 0 Z M 5.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\delta}^{a}}(f_{j+1},\mu_{j+1})
for all
j>p
.
Since
\rho
is accepting in
\mathcal{A}
, eventually there will be a position
k>p
such that

f_{k}(\rho(k))
,
f_{k+1}(\rho(k+1))
,
f_{k+2}(\rho(k+2))
, …are all even (because there is
no model satisfying
\rho
in
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
, so points (iib) and
(iic) from the definition of
\mathrel{\hbox to11.3pt{\vbox to4.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-2.1375pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.29993pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.33 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.78 0 L 1.34 2.03 L 2.92 0 L 1.34 -2.03 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.81 0 M 7.9 0 C 7.9 1.15 6.96 2.09 5.81 2.09 C 4.66 2.09 3.73 1.15 3.73 0 C 3.73 -1.15 4.66 -2.09 5.81 -2.09 C 6.96 -2.09 7.9 -1.15 7.9 0 Z M 5.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\delta}^{a}}
will enforce this).
For the sake of contradiction, assume that
\rho^{\prime\prime}
is accepting.
Then for some position
\ell>k
, because the
i
-component of a macrostate
rotates over all even ranks, it holds that
i_{\ell}=f_{\ell}(\rho(\ell))
and

\rho(\ell)\in O_{\ell}=f_{\ell}^{-1}(\rho(\ell))
.
We can easily show by induction that for all
j\geq\ell
, it holds that

\rho(j)\in O_{j}\neq\emptyset
, which is in contradiction with the
assumption that
\rho^{\prime\prime}
is accepting.

(
\supseteq
)
Consider any word
w\not\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.
From
Corollaries

1
and
4
it follows that the run DAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}
has a bounded rank.
If all vertices of
\mathcal{G}_{w}
are finite, then there is an accepting run
\rho^{\prime}
on
\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})
where
\rho^{\prime}=S_{0}S_{1}\ldots
with
S_{0}=I
and
S_{i+1}=\delta(S_{i},w_{i})
for all
i\in\omega
.
Otherwise,
Algorithm

1
terminates with a tight ranking
r
and a model
m
.
From the definition of
\mathrel{\hbox to11.3pt{\vbox to4.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.3pt\lower-2.1375pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.05 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.29993pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.33 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.78 0 L 1.34 2.03 L 2.92 0 L 1.34 -2.03 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.81 0 M 7.9 0 C 7.9 1.15 6.96 2.09 5.81 2.09 C 4.66 2.09 3.73 1.15 3.73 0 C 3.73 -1.15 4.66 -2.09 5.81 -2.09 C 6.96 -2.09 7.9 -1.15 7.9 0 Z M 5.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\delta}^{a}}
, there is a run

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime\prime}=S_{0}S_{1}\ldots S_{p}(S_{p+1},O_{p+1},f_{p+1},i_{p+1},\mu_{p+1})(S_{p+2},O_{p+2},f_{p+2},i_{p+2},\mu_{p+2})\ldots

such that
f_{k}(q)=r((q,k))
and
\mu_{k}(q)=m((q,k))
for all
k>p
.
In order to show that
\rho^{\prime\prime}
is acepting, we need to show that
the
O
-component of the macrostates on the run is empty infinitely often.
Assume by contradiction that there is an index
\ell>p
such that
O_{j}
is
non-empty for all
j\geq\ell
.
Then, there is a vertex
(q,\ell)\in\mathcal{G}_{w}
s.t.
r((q,\ell))
is even and
there are infinitely many vertices reachable from
(q,\ell)
with the same even
rank, which is a contradiction with the construction of
r
in

Algorithm

1
, which would give some of the vertices odd ranks.
∎

For the complexity analysis, we use
\mathit{tight}(n)
to denote the number of

\mu
-tight level rankings for an automaton with
n
states (
\mu
-tight
rankings for
Inf
-TELAs correspond to
tight
rankings for BAs).
It holds that
\mathit{tight}(n)\approx(0.76n)^{n}

[
13
,
36
]
.

Theorem 3.2

The number of states of
\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})
is in
\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n+1))=\mathcal{O}(n(0.76nk)^{n})
for
k=|\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}|
.

Proof

The set of macrostates
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is obtained by a simple subset construction,
therefore
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\in\mathcal{O}(2^{n})
.
That is much smaller than
\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n+1))
, so it is sufficient to count only the number of macrostates of the form
(S,O,f,i,\mu)
.
For this, we uniquely encode each macrostate as a pair
(h,i)
where
h\colon\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\{-2,-1,\ldots,2n-1\}\times\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
is defined as follows:

h(q)=\begin{cases}(-1,\mu)&\text{if }q\in O,\\
(-2,\mu)&\text{if }q\in\mathcal{Q}\setminus S,\\
(f(q),\mu)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1)

We compute the number of encodings
h
for a fixed
i
.
We divide all encodings into four groups according to the set
\mathrm{img}(h)_{0}\cap\{-2,-1\}
where
\mathrm{img}(h)_{0}
denotes the set of first elements of the pairs in

\mathrm{img}(h)
.
We show that we can obtain the bound

\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
for each of the groups.
The groups are denoted by
g_{M}
with
M\subseteq\{-2,-1\}
.
For
h(q)=(m,\mu)
, we use
h(q)_{m}

and
h(q)_{\mu}
to denote
m
and
\mu
.

g_{\emptyset}
:

from the definition,
f
is
\mu
-tight.
The level model
\mu
is of the form
\mu\colon\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
, so there are
k
possible assignments for every
state from
\mathcal{Q}
.
The number of level models is therefore
k^{n}
and
|g_{\emptyset}|=\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-1\}}
:

since there is at least one state
q
with
h(q)_{m}=-1
,
this means that
q\in O
so
q
has an even rank.
As a consequence, at least one of the positive odd ranks of
h
(up to

2n-1
) will not be taken, so we can infer that
h\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{-1,\ldots,2n-3\}\times\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
.
We can therefore uniquely represent
h
by a mapping
h^{\prime}
by incrementing all
ranks of
h
by two, so
h^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{0,\ldots,2n-1\}\times\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
.
But then
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
and the number of all level models is
k^{n}
, so
|g_{\{-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-2,-1\}}
:

similarly as for
g_{\{-1\}}
we get
that
|g_{\{-2,-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-2\}}
:

the reasoning is similar to the one for
g_{\{-1\}}
,
with the exception that now, we know that there is a state
q\in\mathcal{Q}\setminus S
, which is, according to the definition of a ranking, assigned
the rank
0
.
This means that one positive odd rank of
h
is, again, not taken, so we
increment all non-negative ranks of
h
by two and map all states in
\mathcal{Q}\setminus S
to
1
, obtaining a tight ranking
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
.
The number of level models is
k^{n}
,
therefore,
|g_{\{-2\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

Since the size of all groups is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
, for
a fixed
i
, the total number of these encodings is still

\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.
When we sum the encodings for all
i
’s, we obtain that the number is
bounded by
\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n+1))
, since
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))=\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n+1))

[
36
]
.
The rest follows from the
approximation of
\mathit{tight}(n)
.
∎

Corollary 2

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
Inf
-TELA with
n
states and
k
colours
\Gamma
.
The number of states of
\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})
is in

\mathcal{O}({k\choose\lfloor k/2\rfloor}^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n+1))=\mathcal{O}(n\cdot({k\choose\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\cdot 0.76n)^{n})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(n(2^{k}\cdot 0.76n)^{n})
.

Proof

The proof of the more precise bound follows directly from

Theorem

3.2
and the fact that the size of
\mathcal{M}^{\min}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
is bounded by the size of the largest antichain in
2^{\Gamma}
,
which is at most
{k\choose\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
by Sperner’s theorem.
∎

Corollary 3

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a GBA with
n
states and
k
colours.
Then the number of states of
\textsc{CInfTela}(\mathcal{A})
is in
\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n+1))=\mathcal{O}(n(0.76nk)^{n})
.

Proof

The proof follows directly from
Theorem

3.2
.
For a GBA it holds that
\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}=\bigvee_{0\leq j<k}\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

. The formula is in DNF, hence

. The number of all level models is

.

.

We note that to the best of our knowledge, our bound on the complementation of
GBAs is better than other bounds in the literature.
In particular, it is clearly better than the bound
\mathcal{O}(k^{n}(2n+1)^{n})

from
[
26
]
, which is the best upper bound for complementing GBAs
that we are aware of.
It is also better than an approach that would go through determinization by
using the procedure in
[
37
]
, which outputs a deterministic Rabin
automaton with at most
\mathsf{ghist}_{k}(n)
states and
2^{n}-1
accepting
pairs, which can be complemented easily into a Streett automaton.
According to
[
37
]

\mathsf{ghist}_{k}(n)
converges to
(1.47nk)^{n}

for large
k
, which is already worse than our upper bound.

4
Modular Complementation of
\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\land\varphi
TELAs

In this section, we propose a modular algorithm
\mathsf{FinCompl}
for complementation of
TELAs with the acceptance condition
\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\land\varphi
for
any
\varphi
, parameterized by an algorithm for complementing TELAs
with the condition
\varphi
.
In
Section

5
, we will then instantiate the algorithm for some common
acceptance conditions, eventually obtaining an efficient complementation algorithm for general TELAs.

Let us fix a TELA
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{Q},\delta,I,\Gamma,\mathsf{p},\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\land\varphi)
and let
\Delta
be
\delta
without
transitions whose label contains
c
.
For a word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
, we define a
relaxed run DAG
(RRDAG) over
w
,
denoted by
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
, as
any sequence of states
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}=(S_{0},S_{1},\dots)
where
S_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}
and

\Delta(S_{i},w_{i})\subseteq S_{i+1}
. Intuitively, an RRDAG over a word may contain
more states on each level than it is necessary from the reachability of
\Delta
.
Note that this definition of RRDAGs is equivalent to having vertices of the form
(q,i)
, where

q\in S_{i}
with edges given implicitly by
\Delta
.
We use these definitions interchangeably.
Clearly, there may be multiple RRDAGs over a single word, they are all, however,
subgraphs of the (standard) run DAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}
.
We say that
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}=(S_{0},S_{1},\dots)
is
accepting
wrt
\varphi
,
written as
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}\models\varphi
, if there is a run
\rho=q_{k}q_{k+1}\ldots

for
k\geq 0

in
\Delta
such that for every
i\geq k
it holds that
q_{i}\in S_{i}
and

q_{i+1}\in\Delta(q_{i},w_{i})
, and, moreover,
\rho\models\varphi

(i.e., the accepting run does not need to start at the beginning of
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
).
The reason for introducing RRDAGs is that the algorithm for
condition
\varphi
will construct a BA that runs over RRDAGs constructed using
the restricted transition relation
\Delta
.
The relaxation allows us to introduce new vertices (not connected to the root
of the RRDAG) at any level that represent runs that have seen finitely many
times a
c
transition in
\delta
.

Our definition of the modular procedure
\mathsf{FinCompl}
for

\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\land\varphi
is given wrt
a
subprocedure
for complementing a TELA with condition
\varphi
.
The subprocedure is given as a tuple
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}=(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathsf{SuccAct}_{\Delta},\mathsf{SuccTrack}_{\Delta},\mathsf{EmptyBreak})
, where

(i)

\mathcal{M}
is a set of
macrostates
,

(ii)

\mathcal{M}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
is a set of
initial macrostates
,

(iii)

\mathsf{SuccAct}_{\Delta}\colon 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times\Sigma\times\mathcal{M}\to 2^{\mathcal{M}}
is
an
active transition function
,

(iv)

\mathsf{SuccTrack}_{\Delta}\colon 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times\Sigma\times\mathcal{M}\to 2^{\mathcal{M}}

is a
tracking transition function
, and

(v)

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
is an
empty-breakpoint
predicate.

We use
\mathsf{Succ}_{\Delta}
to denote
\mathsf{SuccAct}_{\Delta}\cup\mathsf{SuccTrack}_{\Delta}

(when treated as relations).
Intuitively,
\mathcal{M}
is a set of macrostates given by the subprocedure
for
\varphi
.

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}
is a condition that has to hold for a macrostate to be accepting in
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
.
The transitions between macrostates of
\mathcal{M}
are described using transition
functions
\mathsf{SuccAct}_{\Delta}
and
\mathsf{SuccTrack}_{\Delta}
.
In particular,
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Succ}_{\Delta}(P^{\prime},a,\mathsf{M})
is computed by taking
the successor of the macrostate
\mathsf{M}
over
a
, but also while taking into
account the set
P^{\prime}
of states (
\mathsf{M}
corresponds to index
i
of the run
while
\mathsf{M}^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
correspond to index
i+1
) provided by
\mathsf{FinCompl}
,
which represent breaking the
\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
condition.
The reason for using two transition functions (
\mathsf{SuccAct}_{\Delta}
and

\mathsf{SuccTrack}_{\Delta}
) is that some subprocedures that
we will introduce later will use two types of macrostates: active and tracking.
For instance, if
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
is a rank-based procedure (cf.
Section

5.2
), active macrostates will contain breakpoint, which the
construction will try to empty, and once a breakpoint is seen,
\mathsf{FinCompl}

will add some more runs to the rank-based algorithm.
The new runs might not be tight at the given point, so we switch into the
tracking mode and wait for newly added runs to become tight before switching
into the active mode again.

Let
w
be a word and
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}=(S_{0},S_{1},\dots)
be an RRDAG over
w
.
A
Fin
-run

R
of
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
over
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
is a sequence

(\mathsf{M}_{0},\mathsf{M}_{1},\dots)
s.t.
\mathsf{M}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
and
\mathsf{M}_{i+1}\in\mathsf{Succ}_{\Delta}(S_{i+1},w_{i},\mathsf{M}_{i})
for all
i\geq 0
.

R
is
accepting
if
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(\mathsf{M}_{i})
holds for
infinitely many
i
’s.
We say that the subprocedure
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
is
correct for
\varphi
if for each
word
w
and every RRDAG
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
over
w
it holds that
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
is
not accepting wrt
\varphi
iff there is an accepting
Fin
-run
R
of

\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
over
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
.

Let us now move to the definition of
\mathsf{FinCompl}
.
For subprocedure
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
and TELA
\mathcal{A}
given above, the
algorithm will construct the BA
\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi},\mathcal{A})=(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},I^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},F^{\prime})
defined as follows:

•

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[t]{ll}\{(S,P,\mathsf{M})\in 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times\mathcal{M}\},\end{array}

•

I^{\prime}=\{(I,I,\mathsf{M}_{0})\mid\mathsf{M}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}_{0}\}
,

•

\delta^{\prime}=\delta_{1}\cup\delta_{2}
where

–

\delta_{1}\colon\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\times\Sigma\to 2^{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
such that
(S^{\prime},P^{\prime},\mathsf{M}^{\prime})\in\delta_{1}((S,P,\mathsf{M}),a)
iff

*

S^{\prime}=\delta(S,a)
,

*

if
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(\mathsf{M})
:
P^{\prime}=S^{\prime}
,

*

if
\neg\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(\mathsf{M})
:
P^{\prime}=\Delta(P,a)
,

*

\mathsf{M}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SuccAct}_{\Delta}(P^{\prime},a,\mathsf{M})
,

–

\delta_{2}\colon\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\times\Sigma\to 2^{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
such that
(S^{\prime},P^{\prime},\mathsf{M}^{\prime})\in\delta_{2}((S,P,\mathsf{M}),a)
iff

*

S^{\prime}=\delta(S,a)
,

*

P^{\prime}=\Delta(P,a)
,

*

\mathsf{M}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SuccTrack}_{\Delta}(P^{\prime},a,\mathsf{M})
, and

•

F^{\prime}=\{(S,P,\mathsf{M})\overset{a}{\rightarrow}(S^{\prime},P^{\prime},\mathsf{M}^{\prime})\in\delta^{\prime}\mid a\in\Sigma,\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(\mathsf{M}^{\prime})\}
.

Intuitively, the construction executes
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
on the restricted
transition relation
\Delta
, while also keeping track of all runs (in
S
) and
runs that either need to terminate or see a
c
-transition (in
P
).
Whenever
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
clears its breakpoint,
P
is re-sampled (and
some new runs can be added to
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
).

Theorem 4.1

For a correct subprocedure
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
,

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi},\mathcal{A}))=\Sigma^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

The overhead of the procedure over the subprocedure
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
is at most
3^{n}
-times.

Theorem 4.2

Suppose
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}=(\mathcal{M},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
.
Then
|\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi},\mathcal{A})|\in\mathcal{O}(3^{n}\cdot|\mathcal{M}|)
.

Proof

Since in
(S,P,\mathsf{M})
, it always holds that
P\subseteq S
, each state of
\mathcal{A}
can be in one of the three following sets:

(i)

\mathcal{Q}\setminus S
,

(ii)

S\cap P
, and

(iii)

S\setminus P
.

∎

5
Complementation of TELAs and their Subclasses

We proceed by instantiating the modular algorithm
\mathsf{FinCompl}
from the
previous section for several common automata classes—co-Büchi automata,
Rabin automata, parity automata, generalized Rabin automata, and, eventually,
TELAs.

5.1
Co-Büchi Automata

As a simple demonstration of instantiation of
\mathsf{FinCompl}
, we use it to create
a complementation algorithm for co-Büchi automata.
The acceptance condition for co-Büchi automata is
\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
