\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.20001pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.04 -3.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})=\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.20001pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.04 -3.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\land\mathit{tt}
, we therefore need to provide a trivial
subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}}=(\mathcal{M}^{\mathit{tt}},\mathcal{M}^{\mathit{tt}}_{0},\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\mathit{tt}}_{\Delta},\emptyset,\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\mathit{tt}})
that is correct for
\mathit{tt}
(notice that
\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\mathit{tt}}_{\Delta}
is empty).
In the subprocedure,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathit{tt}}=2^{\mathcal{Q}}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathit{tt}}_{0}=\{I\}
, and
the remaining components are given as follows:

\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\mathit{tt}}_{\Delta}(P,a,S)=\{P\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\mathit{tt}}(P)\Longleftrightarrow P=\emptyset.

Intuitively, the instantiated procedure works with macrostates
(S,P,P)
(i.e.,
to adhere to the formal definition of
\mathsf{FinCompl}
,
P

is there twice) where
S
tracks all runs and
P
is a breakpoint that contains
runs that yet need to either terminate or see
0
.
To accept,
P
needs to be emptied infinitely often.
One can observe that
\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}},\mathcal{A})
resembles the well-known
Miyano-Hayashi construction
[
32
]
for complementation of
co-Büchi automata.

Lemma 5

The subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}}
is correct for the acceptance condition
\mathit{tt}
.

Corollary 4

For a co-Büchi automaton
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}},\mathcal{A}))=\Sigma^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof

Follows from
Lemmas

5
and
4.1
.
∎

Since the result of the construction can be mapped to the Miyano-Hayashi’s
algorithm
[
32
]
, the complexities also match.

Corollary 5

|\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}},\mathcal{A})|\in\mathcal{O}(3^{n})

5.2
Rabin Automata

In this section, we give an instantiation of
\mathsf{FinCompl}
with subprocedure

\mathbb{S}^{\mathsf{inf}}=(\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{0}
,
\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}
,

\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta},\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\mathsf{inf}})
for
\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FF4DA0} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FF4DA0} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.20001pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.04 -3.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
, which will allow us to complement TELAs
where the acceptance condition is a single Rabin pair.
The algorithm is based on the optimal rank-based BA complementation algorithm
from
[
36
]
adjusted to the needs of the modular construction.
The macrostates of the instantiation
are given as

\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}=\overset{\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\mathsf{Act}}}{\overbrace{2^{\mathcal{Q}}\cup(\mathcal{T}\times 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times\{0,2,\ldots,2n-2\})}}\cup\overset{\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\mathsf{Track}}}{\overbrace{(\mathcal{T}\times\{0,2,\ldots,2n-2\})}}

where
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{0}=\{I\}
.
Notice that
active macrostates
(
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\mathsf{Act}}
) are either sets of states
(from
2^{\mathcal{Q}}
, just keeping track of all runs) or states of the form
(f,O,i)
(representing tight runs).
On the other hand,
tracking macrostates
(
\mathcal{M}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\mathsf{Track}}
) are of the form

(f,i)
; these are used to wait for newly arrived runs to become tight.
The remaining components are then defined as follows:

•

(f^{\prime},O^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,(f,O,i))
iff

–

f\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}^{a}f^{\prime}
and
\mathit{rank}(f)=\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})
,

–

\text{dom}(f^{\prime})=P
,

–

O\neq\emptyset
,

–

i^{\prime}=i
,

–

O^{\prime}=\Delta(O,a)\cap f^{\prime-1}(i)

•

(f^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,(f,O,i))
iff

–

f\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}^{a}f^{\prime}
and
\mathit{rank}(f)=\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})
,

–

O=\emptyset
,

–

i^{\prime}=(i+2)\mod(\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})+1)

•

P^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,P)
iff

–

P^{\prime}=P

•

(f^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,P)
iff

–

f^{\prime}
is
P
-tight

–

i^{\prime}=0

•

\{(f^{\prime},i^{\prime}),(f^{\prime},O^{\prime},i^{\prime})\}\subseteq\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,(f,i))
iff

–

f\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}^{a}f^{\prime}
and
\mathit{rank}(f)=\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})
,

–

O^{\prime}=f^{\prime-1}(i^{\prime})
,

–

i^{\prime}=i

•

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\mathsf{inf}}((f,O,i))\Longleftrightarrow O=\emptyset

•

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\mathsf{inf}}(P)\Longleftrightarrow P=\emptyset

•

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\mathsf{inf}}((f,i))\Longleftrightarrow\mathit{false}

An example of the construction is shown in
Appendix

0.C
.
The correctness of the instantiation is then summarized by the following lemma.

Lemma 6

The subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\mathsf{inf}}
is correct for the acceptance condition
\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FF4DA0} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FF4DA0} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.12503pt}{-2.57777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.94 -3.57)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
.

The following lemma shows that using our approach, handling the

\textrm{Fin}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#505050} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.84076pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.55 -2.38)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
condition is “
for free
,” i.e., the
asymptotical complexity stays the same as for the optimal algorithm for BA
complementation from
[
36
]
.

Lemma 7

|\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}^{\mathsf{inf}},\mathcal{A})|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n+1))
.

Proof

It suffices to count the number of macrostates of the form
(S,P,f,O,i)
.
Consider a macrostate
(S,P,f,O,i)
. We uniquely encode the macrostate as

(h,i)
where
h\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{-3,\ldots,2n-1\}
is defined as follows:

h(q)=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if }q\in O,\\
-2&\text{if }q\in\mathcal{Q}\setminus S,\\
-3&\text{if }q\in S\setminus P,\text{ and}\\
f(q)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2)

For a fixed
i
we compute the number of such encodings
h
.
First we divide all encodings into groups according to the set
\mathrm{img}(h)\cap\{-3,-2,-1\}
(8 groups at most) and we will show for each of the
groups how we can “
shuffle
” the ranks in
h
to obtain the bound

\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
for each of the groups.
We will denote each of the groups by
g_{M}
with
M\subseteq\{-3,-2,-1\}
.

g_{\emptyset}
:

from the definition,
f
is tight so
|g_{\emptyset}|=\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))

g_{\{-1\}}
:

since there is at least one state
q
with
h(q)=-1
,
this means that
q\in O
so
q
has an even rank.
As a consequence, at least one of the positive odd ranks of
h
will not
be taken, so we can infer that
h\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{-1,\ldots,2n-3\}
.
We can therefore uniquely map
h
to a mapping
h^{\prime}
by incrementing all
ranks of
h
by two, so
h^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{1,\ldots,2n-1\}
.
But then
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
, so
|g_{\{-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-2,-1\}}
:

via the same reasoning as for
g_{\{-1\}}
we get
that
|g_{\{-2,-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-2\}}
:

the reasoning is similar to the one for
g_{\{-1\}}
,
with the exception that now, we know that there is a state
q\in\mathcal{Q}\setminus S
, which is, according to the definition of a ranking, assigned
the rank
0
.
This means that one positive odd rank of
h
is, again, not taken, so we
increment all non-negative ranks of
h
by two and map all states in
\mathcal{Q}\setminus S
to
1
, obtaining a tight ranking
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
.
Therefore,
|g_{\{-2\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-3\}}
:

the reasoning is, again, similar to the one for
g_{\{-1\}}
,
with the exception that now, we know that there is a state
q\in S\setminus P
such that its rank is, according to the definition
0
.
Therefore, we increment all non-negative ranks of
h
by two and map the
states in
S\setminus P
to
1
, obtaining a tight ranking
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
;
therefore,
|g_{\{-3\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-3,-2\}}
,
g_{\{-3,-1\}}
:

similarly as for
g_{\{-2\}}
, we increment all non-negative ranks of
h

by two and set
h^{\prime}(q)=0
if
h(q)=-3
and
h^{\prime}(q)=1
if
h(q)=-2

(resp. if
h(q)=-1
).
Then
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
and so
|g_{\{-3,-2\}}|=\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
and

|g_{\{-3,-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-3,-2,-1\}}
:

in this case, we know that there is at least
one state
q_{1}\in O
and at least one state
q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}\setminus S
.
Therefore, there will be at least two odd positions not taken in
h
, so
we can infer that
h\colon\{-3,\ldots,2n-5\}
.
We create
h^{\prime}
by incrementing all ranks in
h
by
four
; in this
way, we obtain a tight ranking
h^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{0,\ldots,2n-1\}
, so

|g_{\{-3,-2,-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
.

Since the size of all groups is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
, for
a fixed
i
, the total number of these encodings is still

\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n))
.
When we sum the encodings for all possible
i
’s, we obtain that the number is
bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n+1))
, since
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))=\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n+1))

[
36
]
.
∎

The modular construction instantiated with
\mathbb{S}^{\mathsf{inf}}
gives us a procedure for
the complementation of Rabin automata with a single pair.
In order to get a procedure for general Rabin automata, we construct
a complement automaton for each Rabin pair and make a product of these automata
and obtain a GBA accepting the complement of the original Rabin automaton.
The complexity reasoning is then straightforward and is summarized by the
following corollary.

Corollary 6

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a Rabin automaton with
k
Rabin pairs.
Then we can construct a GBA accepting the complement of the language
of
\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n+1)^{k})=\mathcal{O}(n^{k}(0.76n)^{nk})
states and
k

colours.

Proof

​​
\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n\!+\!1)^{k})\!=\!\mathcal{O}(\!(n\!\cdot\!\mathit{tight}(n)\!)^{k})\!=\!\mathcal{O}(\!(n(0.76n)^{n})^{k})\!=\!\mathcal{O}(n^{k}(0.76n)^{nk})

∎

To the best of our knowledge, the state complexity of our procedure is
better than the complexity of other approaches (even if we require the output
to be a BA and not a GBA).
In particular, it is better than the complexity
\mathcal{O}(k\cdot 3^{n}\cdot(2n+1)^{nk})
of
[
24
]
and also better than the complexity of a procedure
that would first transform the input Rabin automaton into a BA with
m=nk
states
and run the optimal BA complementation
with complexity
\mathcal{O}(m(0.76m)^{m})=\mathcal{O}(nk(0.76nk)^{nk})

[
36
]
,

as shown by the following lemmas.

Lemma 8

\mathcal{O}(n^{k}(0.76n)^{nk})\subset\mathcal{O}(k\cdot 3^{n}\cdot(2n+1)^{nk})

Proof

n^{k}(0.76n)^{nk}=(\sqrt[n]{n}\cdot 0.76n)^{nk}
. The global maximum of the
function
\sqrt[n]{n}
is less than 1.5, so
(\sqrt[n]{n}\cdot 0.76n)^{nk}<(1.14n)^{nk}<(2n+1)^{nk}
for
n\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 9

\mathcal{O}(n^{k}(0.76n)^{nk})\subset\mathcal{O}(nk(0.76nk)^{nk})

Proof

Similar reasoning as in the proof of
Lemma

8
.
∎

5.3
Parity Automata

Since the parity condition is a special case of the Rabin
condition
[
14
]
, we can easily give an upper bound on the
complementation of parity automata.

Lemma 10

For a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
with index
k
,
there is a GBA for the complement of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with
​
\frac{k}{2}
​ colours and
\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n\!+\!1)^{\frac{k}{2}})\!=\!\mathcal{O}(n^{\frac{k}{2}}(0.76n)^{\frac{nk}{2}})
states.

Proof

The min-odd parity acceptance condition is of the form

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#E31A1C} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#E31A1C} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.20001pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.04 -3.84)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}))\lor\ldots

which is a Rabin acceptance condition with
\frac{k}{2}
Rabin pairs.
Note that we can use a new colour for each union of colours and we obtain the same number of colours
as in
\mathsf{Acc}
.
According to
Corollary

6
, the parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
can be complemented into a GBA
with
\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n+1)^{\frac{k}{2}})
states.
∎

We note that the complexity obtained by our general procedure is worse than the
best one we are aware of, which is
2^{\mathcal{O}(n\log n)}

[
7
]
.

.
We can now easily combine the procedure for (standard) Rabin automata from the
previous section and the procedure for
Inf
-TELA from
Section

3.2

to construct the subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}
for
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

.
The set of macrostates will be

\mathcal{M}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}=2^{\mathcal{Q}}\cup(\mathcal{T}\times 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times\{0,2,\dots,2n-2\}\times\mathsf{LM})\cup(\mathcal{T}\times\{0,2,\dots,2n-2\}\times\mathsf{LM})

Details are given in
Appendix

0.D
.
Similarly to
Sections

3.2
and
5.2
, one can then obtain the following
bound on the size of the complement.

Lemma 11

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a generalized Rabin automaton with one generalized Rabin pair
with
\ell

Inf
s. Then, there exists a BA accepting the complement
of
\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{n}\mathit{tight}(n+1))=\mathcal{O}(n\ell^{n}(0.76n)^{n})
states.

Theorem 5.1

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a generalized Rabin automaton with
k
generalized Rabin pairs, each
with at most
\ell

Inf
s. Then, there exists a GBA with
k
colours and

\mathcal{O}(\ell^{nk}\mathit{tight}(n+1)^{k})=\mathcal{O}(n^{k}(0.76\ell n)^{nk})
states
accepting
\Sigma^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

There is not much work on the complementation of generalized Rabin automata or
general TELAs (we are only aware of the upper bound
2^{2^{\mathcal{O}(n)}}

from
[
35
]
)).
One could approach the complementation by translation of the generalized Rabin automaton into a GBA
using the technique from
[
20
]
.
The technique first performs
Fin
-removal, i.e., it makes
k
copies
of
\mathcal{A}
, each with the corresponding
Fin
-transitions removed, obtaining
a GBA with
n(k+1)
states and
\ell
colours (one can share colours across the
independent copies).
After that, we could use our GBA complementation algorithm from

Section

3
, which would give us a BA with

\mathcal{O}(n(k+1)(0.76\ell n(k+1))^{n(k+1)})
states, which is worse.

Lemma 12

\mathcal{O}(n^{k}(0.76\ell n)^{nk})\subset\mathcal{O}(n(k+1)(0.76\ell n(k+1))^{n(k+1)})

Proof
(Idea)

Let us observe the behaviour of the fraction with a simplified right-hand side:

\frac{nk(0.76\ell nk)^{nk}}{n^{k}(0.76\ell n)^{nk}}=\frac{nk^{nk+1}}{n^{k}}
.
There are two options:

(i)

n\geq k
: in this case,
k^{nk}\gg n^{k}
and the theorem holds.

(ii)

k\geq n
: in this case,
k^{k}\gg n^{k}
and the theorem holds.
∎

5.5
General TELAs

For complementation of general TELAs, we use the fact that any TELA can be
converted into a generalized Rabin automaton with the same structure by
modifying the acceptance condition into the DNF form (and not touching the
structure of the automaton).
For a TELA with
k
colours, the DNF will have at most
2^{k}
clauses (i.e.,
generalized Rabin pairs), each one with at most
k
literals.

Theorem 5.2

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a TELA with
k
colours.
Then, there exists a GBA with
2^{k}
colours and

\mathcal{O}(k^{n2^{k}}\mathit{tight}(n+1)^{2^{k}})=\mathcal{O}(n^{2^{k}}(0.76nk)^{n2^{k}})
states
accepting
\Sigma^{\omega}\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof

By substituting to
Theorem

5.1
.
∎

6
Related Work

Lower bounds for complementation of classes of
\omega
-automata using the full
automata technique were established in
[
43
]
(improving the previous

\Omega(n!)
lower bound of Michel
[
31
]
).
The technique was later generalized to improve the lower bound of Rabin
automata complementation
[
8
]
.
Double exponential lower bound for complementation of general Emerson-Lei automata was
given in
[
35
]
.

See the survey in
[
4
]
for more details.

Simultaneously to establishing the lower bound, there emerged algorithms for determinizing and complementing various classes of
\omega
-automata.
The optimal determinization approach for GBAs introduced in
[
37
]
yields deterministic Rabin automaton of
\mathsf{ghist}_{k}(n)
states
and
2^{n}-1
Rabin pairs, where
\mathsf{ghist}_{k}(n)
converges against
(1.47nk)^{n}
for large
k
.
Rank-based complementation of GBAs was proposed in
[
26
]
. Furthermore,
there are approaches for semideterminization-based complementation of
GBAs
[
3
]
with double exponential complexity.
Regarding other acceptance conditions, determinization of parity automata based on root history trees was proposed in
[
38
]
.
A rank-based complementation of Streett and Rabin automata was introduced in
[
24
]
and later
improved by tree structures in
[
7
]
. Tight determinization of Streett automata was presented in
[
41
]
.
Tight complementation technique for parity automata based on flattened nested history trees was then proposed in
[
39
]
.
A lot of effort has been put into complementation of Büchi automata leading to algorithms roughly divided into several groups:
Ramsey-based
[
5
,
6
,
40
]
,
rank-based
[
15
,
18
,
17
,
42
,
25
,
36
]
,
determinization-based
[
34
,
33
,
28
]
, slice-based
[
21
]
, and others
[
1
,
16
,
29
]
.
There are specialized more efficient algorithms for subclasses of
BAs, such as inherently-weaks
[
32
]
, deterministic
[
27
]
,
semideterministic
[
2
]
,
elevator
[
18
,
16
]
, or
unambiguous
[
30
,
12
]
BAs.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
, the
transition function
\delta
and the transition function
\Delta
omitting
transitions labelled by
2
(i.e., the single transition
r\overset{a}{\rightarrow}t
).
Then, for a word
w=(cab)^{\omega}
, we show a particular run DAG in each subfigure.

[OL: ]

Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\omega
be a set of indices.
An RRDAG
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}=(S_{0},S_{1},\dots)
is
\mathcal{I}
-narrow if
S_{0}=I
and for all

i\in\omega
it holds that

(i)

\Delta(S_{i},w_{i})=S_{i+1}
if
i+1\notin\mathcal{I}
and

(ii)

\Delta(S_{i},w_{i})\subseteq S_{i+1}
otherwise.

An RRDAG is called
narrow
if it is
\emptyset
-narrow (note that for
every word, there is exactly one narrow RRDAG).
Let
\mathcal{G}_{1}=(S_{0},S_{1},\dots)
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}=(S_{0}^{\prime},S_{1}^{\prime},\dots)
be two RRDAGs.
We say that for a set of indices
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{G}_{1}
matches
\mathcal{G}_{2}
on
\mathcal{I}
if

S_{i}^{\prime}=S_{i}
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
.
An example illustrating the notions is given in
Fig.

3
.
We say that an
\mathcal{I}
-narrow RRDAG
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
is
accepting
wrt
\varphi
if
it matches the (standard) run DAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}
on
\mathcal{I}
and there is some
k\geq 0
such that
there is a run
\rho=q_{k}q_{k+1}\ldots
with
\rho\models\varphi
and for all
i\geq k
it holds that
q_{i}\in S_{i}
and
q_{i+1}\in\Delta(q_{i},w_{i})
.
Intuitively,
\mathcal{I}
will be positions where we re-sample
P
, so the level will
be the same as in the full run DAG.

Lemma 14

Let
w
be a word and
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
be the narrow RRDAG.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
be two infinite sets of indices and
\mathcal{H}^{\Delta}_{w}

and
\mathcal{K}^{\Delta}_{w}
be two
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}
-narrow (
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
-narrow) RRDAGs
that match
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}
(
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
).
Then,
\mathcal{H}^{\Delta}_{w}
is accepting wrt
\varphi
iff
\mathcal{K}^{\Delta}_{w}
is accepting wrt
\varphi
.

Proof

(
\Rightarrow
)
If
\mathcal{H}_{w}^{\Delta}=(H_{0},H_{1},\ldots)
is accepting wrt
\varphi
, then there is some
k\geq 0
such that there is a run
\rho=q_{k}q_{k+1}\ldots
with
\rho\models\varphi
and for all
i\geq k
it holds that
q_{i}\in H_{i}
and
q_{i+1}\in\Delta(q_{i},w_{i})
.
Since
\mathcal{H}_{w}^{\Delta}
matches
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}=(S_{0},S_{1},\ldots)
on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}
, there is some
l\geq k
such that
l\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}}
and
H_{l}=S_{l}
. Since
\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\Delta}=(K_{0},K_{1},\ldots)
matches
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
on
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
, there is some
m\geq l
such that
m\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
K_{m}=S_{m}
.
That means that there is an accepting run
\rho^{\prime}=q_{m}q_{m+1}\ldots
and
\mathcal{K}_{w}^{\Delta}
is accepting wrt
\varphi
.

(
\Leftarrow
) A similar reasoning as in (
\Rightarrow
) can be used.
∎

\Leftarrow
)
Since all runs in the narrow RRDAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
are also present in

and

is not accepting,

is also not accepting.
∎

Lemma 16

Let
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi}
be a correct subprocedure,
w
be a word, and

\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}=(S_{0},S_{1},\dots)
be the narrow RRDAG.
Furthermore, let

\rho=(S_{0},P_{0},\mathsf{M}_{0})(S_{1},P_{1},\mathsf{M}_{1})\ldots
be a run of
\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}^{\varphi},\mathcal{A})
over
w
and
\mathcal{I}=\{i\in\omega\mid S_{i}=P_{i}\}
.
Then
\mathcal{G}_{\rho}^{\Delta}=(P_{0},P_{1},\dots)
is an
\mathcal{I}
-narrow RRDAG that matches
\mathcal{G}^{\Delta}_{w}
on
\mathcal{I}
.

Proof

In order to show that
\mathcal{G}_{\rho}^{\Delta}
is an
\mathcal{I}
-narrow run DAG that matches
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
on
\mathcal{I}
, we need to show that for indices
i+1\in\mathcal{I}
it holds that
\Delta(P_{i},w_{i})\subseteq P_{i+1}
, for indices
i+1\not\in\mathcal{I}
it holds that
\Delta(P_{i},w_{i})=P_{i+1}
and for
i\in\mathcal{I}
it holds that
S_{i}=P_{i}
.
If
i+1\in\mathcal{I}
, then
P_{i+1}=S_{i+1}
. Since
P_{i}\subseteq S_{i}
, it holds that
\Delta(P_{i},w_{i})\subseteq P_{i+1}
.
If
i+1\not\in\mathcal{I}
, then
S_{i+1}\neq P_{i+1}
, therefore
P_{i+1}=\Delta(P,a)
(follows directly from the procedure).

\mathcal{G}_{\rho}^{\Delta}
matches
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\delta}
on
\mathcal{I}
because for every
i\in\mathcal{I}
it trivially holds that
S_{i}=P_{i}
.
∎

See
4.1

Proof

. Then there is an accepting run

and we can again apply
Lemma

15
to get an RRDAG
\mathcal{G}_{w[i:]}^{\Delta}
, which is not accepting wrt
\varphi
. We can hence repeatedly construct another run of
\mathbb{S}_{\Delta}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w[i:]}^{\delta}
and mimic the construction of another part of
R
in the same way as depicted. From the construction,
R
is accepting meaning that
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FinCompl}(\mathbb{S}_{\Delta},\mathcal{A}))
.
∎

Appendix 0.B
Proofs for
Section

5

See
5

Proof

In order to show that the subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}}
is correct for
\mathit{tt}
, we need to show that for each word
w
and every RRDAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
it holds that
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is not accepting wrt
\mathit{tt}
iff there is an accepting
\mathsf{Fin}
-run of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
.
We first prove this statement from left to right. Assume that
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}=(S_{1},S_{2},\ldots)
is not accepting wrt
\mathit{tt}
, i.e., there is no run
\rho=q_{k}q_{k+1}\ldots
for
k\geq 0
such that for every
i\geq k
it holds that
q_{i}\in S_{i}
and
q_{i+1}\in\Delta(q_{i},w_{i})
and
\rho\models\mathit{tt}
.
Then, a
\mathsf{Fin}
-run R of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is a sequence
(M_{0},M_{1},\ldots)
such that
M_{0}=I
and for all
i
it holds that if
M_{i}=\emptyset
, then
M_{i+1}=S_{i+1}
and if
M_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then
M_{i+1}=\Delta(M_{i},w_{i})
.
Since all runs on the input automaton contain infinitely many
c
-transitions, for all
k>0
, there is some
j>k
such that
M_{j}=\emptyset
and
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(M_{j})
is true. The
\mathsf{Fin}
-run R is therefore accepting.

Now we prove the statement from right to left. Consider an accepting
\mathsf{Fin}
-run
R=(M_{0},M_{1},\ldots)
of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathit{tt}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
. It holds that
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(M_{i})
is true for infinitely many
i
’s. That means that all sampled runs eventually end when using
\Delta
as a transition function, because all runs contain infinitely many accepting states. The word
w
is therefore not accepted by the input automaton and
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is not accepting.
∎

.
There is either no run of
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
on

at all or all runs do not satisfy the formula. If there is no run of
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
on

, then there is a sequence

and

, so it holds infinitely often, and there therefore exists an accepting run of

over

does not hold for the particular run. With every transition

we sample all currently reachable states and then check that all runs from these states contain transitions from

only finitely often by modified Schewe’s rank-based algorithm. The

-component is emptied infinitely often and there is therefore an accepting run of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathsf{inf}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
.

Now we prove the equivalence in the opposite direction. Assume that there is an accepting run of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathsf{inf}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
. There is therefore a run where the
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}
predicate is true infinitely many times.
The first possible option is that
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(P)
is true infinitely many times. That can happen only if there is no run on
w
and
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is finite. If there is no such run, the formula is not satisfied and
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is not accepting.
The second option is that
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}((f,O,i))
is true infinitely many times. That means that the formula
\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

does not hold for any run, no matter when the run stops containing transitions from

Appendix 0.D
Generalized Rabin Automata

In this section, we give a subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}
for
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.9493pt}{-1.86658pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.7 -2.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
of the modular procedure with an
algorithm allowing to complement generalized Rabin automata automata with a single generalized Rabin pair.
The macrostates are given as
\mathcal{M}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}=2^{\mathcal{Q}}\cup(\mathcal{T}\times\{0,2,\dots,2n-2\}\times\mathsf{LM})\cup(\mathcal{T}\times 2^{\mathcal{Q}}\times\{0,2,\dots,2n-2\}\times\mathsf{LM})
where
|\mathcal{Q}|=n
and
\mathcal{M}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{0}=\{I\}
.
The components are then defined as follows:

•

(f^{\prime},O^{\prime},i^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,(f,O,i,\mu))
iff

–

f\sqsubseteq_{\Delta,\mu,\mu^{\prime}}^{a}f^{\prime}
and
\mathit{rank}(f)=\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})
,

–

\text{dom}(f^{\prime})=P
,

–

O\neq\emptyset

–

i^{\prime}=i
,

–

O^{\prime}=\delta(O,a)\cap f^{\prime-1}(i)

•

(f^{\prime},i^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,(f,O,i,\mu))
iff

–

f\sqsubseteq_{\Delta,\mu,\mu^{\prime}}^{a}f^{\prime}
and
\mathit{rank}(f)=\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})
,

–

O=\emptyset

–

i^{\prime}=(i+2)\mod(\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})+1)

•

P^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,P)
iff

–

P^{\prime}=P

•

P^{\prime}\in\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,P)
iff

–

P^{\prime}=P

•

(f^{\prime},i^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a)
iff

–

f^{\prime}
is
(P,\mu^{\prime})
-tight

–

\mu^{\prime}\in\mathsf{LM}
,

–

i^{\prime}=0

•

\{(f^{\prime},O^{\prime},i^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}),(f^{\prime},i^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\}\subseteq\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta}(P,a,(f,i,\mu))
iff

–

f\sqsubseteq_{\Delta,\mu,\mu^{\prime}}^{a}f^{\prime}
and
\mathit{rank}(f)=\mathit{rank}(f^{\prime})
,

–

O^{\prime}=f^{\prime-1}(i)
,

–

\mu^{\prime}\in\mathsf{LM}
,

–

i^{\prime}=i

•

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}((f,O,i,\mu))\Longleftrightarrow O=\emptyset

•

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}(P)\Longleftrightarrow P=\emptyset

•

\neg\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\mathsf{inf}}((f,i,\mu))

Lemma 17

The subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}=(\mathsf{M}^{\land\mathsf{inf}},\mathsf{M}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{0},\mathsf{SuccAct}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta},\mathsf{SuccTrack}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}_{\Delta},\mathsf{EmptyBreak}^{\land\mathsf{inf}})
for
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.9493pt}{-1.86658pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.7 -2.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
is correct.

Proof
(Sketch)

In order to show that the subprocedure
\mathbb{S}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}
is correct, we need to show that for each word
w
and every RRDAG
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\delta}
over
w
it holds that
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\delta}
is not accepting wrt
\varphi
iff there exists an accepting run of
\mathbb{S}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
.
We begin with the proof of the equivalence from left to right.
Assume that
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is not accepting.
There is either no run on
w
at all or all runs do not satisfy the formula. If there is no run of
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
on
w
, there is a sequence
(M_{0},M_{1},\ldots)
where
M_{0}=I
and
M_{j+1}=\Delta(M_{j},a)
for all
j\geq 0
such that there is some
i\geq 0
such that
M_{l}=\emptyset
for all
l\geq i
. The predicate
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(M_{l})
is true for all
l\geq i
and there therefore exists an accepting run of
\mathbb{S}_{\delta}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
.
Now assume that there is a run of
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
on
w
. Then, no matter from which point there are no transitions from
c
, the condition
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.9493pt}{-1.86658pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.7 -2.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
does not hold for the particular run. With every transition
(f,i,\mu)\rightarrow(f^{\prime},O^{\prime},i^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
we sample all currently reachable states and then check that the condition
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.9493pt}{-1.86658pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.7 -2.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
does not hold for any run from these states by modified Schewe’s rank-based algorithm for GBAs described in
Section

3.2
. The
O
-component is emptied infinitely often and there is therefore an accepting run of
\mathbb{S}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
.

Now we prove the equivalence in the opposite direction. Assume that there is an accepting run of
\mathbb{S}^{\land\mathsf{inf}}
over
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
. There is therefore a run where the
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}
predicate is true infinitely many times.
The first possible option is that

\mathsf{EmptyBreak}(P)
is true infinitely many times. That can happen only if there is no run on
w
and
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is finite. If there is no such run, the formula is not satisfied and
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is not accepting.
The second option is that
\mathsf{EmptyBreak}((f,O,i))
is true infinitely many times. That means that the formula
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{Inf}(\hbox to9.39pt{\vbox to9.39pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.6947pt\lower-4.6947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}
\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#1F78B4} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B2A4FF} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.5 0 C 6.5 3.59 3.59 6.5 0 6.5 C -3.59 6.5 -6.5 3.59 -6.5 0 C -6.5 -3.59 -3.59 -6.5 0 -6.5 C 3.59 -6.5 6.5 -3.59 6.5 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.9493pt}{-1.86658pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.7 -2.58)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})
does not hold for any run, no matter when the run stops containing transitions from
c
. The formula is therefore not satisfied in any run and
\mathcal{G}_{w}^{\Delta}
is not accepting.
∎

Lemma 18

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a generalized Rabin automaton with one generalized Rabin pair with
\ell

Inf
s. Then, the
complemented GBA has
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{n}\mathit{tight}(n+1))
states.

Proof

It suffices to count the number of macrostates of the form
(S,P,f,O,i,\mu)
. Consider a macrostate
(S,P,f,O,i,\mu)
.
We uniquely encode each macrostate as
(h,i)
where
h\colon\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\{-3,-2,-1,\ldots,2n-1\}\times\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
for
n=|\mathcal{Q}|
is defined as follows:

h(q)=\begin{cases}(-1,\mu)&\text{if }q\in O,\\
(-2,\mu)&\text{if }q\in\mathcal{Q}\setminus S,\\
(-3,\mu)&\text{if }q\in S\setminus P,\text{and}\\
(f(q),\mu)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(3)

We compute the number of encodings
h
for a fixed
i
. We divide all encodings into groups according to the set
img(h)_{0}\cap\{-3,-2,-1\}
where
img(h)_{0}
denotes the set of first elements of the pairs in
img(h)
. We show that for each of the (at most 8) groups we can obtain the bound
\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.
Each of the group is denoted by
g_{M}
with
M\subseteq\{-2,-1\}
,
i.e.,
g_{M}=\{h\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{-3,-2,\ldots,2n-1\}\times\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}\mid M=\mathrm{img}(h)_{0}\cap\{-3,-2,-1\}\}
.
For
h(q)=(m,\mu)
, we denote
m
by
h(q)(0)
and
\mu
by
h(q)(1)
.

g_{\emptyset}
:

from the definition,
f
is
\mu
-tight. The level model
\mu
is of the form
\mu\colon\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
, so there are
l
possible assignments for every state from
\mathcal{Q}
. The number of level models is therefore
l^{n}
and
|g_{\emptyset}|=\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-1\}}
:

since there is at least one state
q
with
h(q)(0)=-1
,
this means that
q\in O
so
q
has an even rank.
As a consequence, at least one of the positive odd ranks of
h
will not
be taken, so we can infer that
h\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{-1,\ldots,2n-3\}\times\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
.
We can therefore uniquely map
h
to a mapping
h^{\prime}
by incrementing all
ranks of
h
by two, so
h^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{0,\ldots,2n-1\}\times\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathsf{Acc}}}
.
But then
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
and the number of all level models is
l^{n}
, so
|g_{\{-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-2,-1\}}
:

via the same reasoning as for
g_{\{-1\}}
we get
that
|g_{\{-2,-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-2\}}
:

the reasoning is similar to the one for
g_{\{-1\}}
,
with the exception that now, we know that there is a state
q\in Q\setminus S
, which is, according to the definition of a ranking, assigned
the rank
0
.
This means that one positive odd rank of
h
is, again, not taken, so we
increment all non-negative ranks of
h
by two and map all states in
Q\setminus S
to
1
, obtaining a tight ranking
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
.
The number of level models is
l^{n}
,
therefore,
|g_{\{-2\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-3\}}
:

the reasoning is similar to the one for
g_{\{-1\}}
, with the exception that now we know that there is some state
q\in S\setminus P
such that its rank is, according to the definition,
0
. Therefore, we increment all non-negative ranks of
h
by two and map the states in
P\setminus\mathcal{Q}
to 1, obtaining a tight ranking
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
. For
l^{n}
possible level models, it holds that
|g_{\{-3\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-3,-2\}}
,
g_{\{-3,-1\}}
:

similarly as for
g_{\{-2\}}
, we increment all non-negative ranks of
h

by two and set
h^{\prime}(q)(0)=0
if
h(q)(0)=-3
and
h^{\prime}(q)(0)=1
if
h(q)(0)=-2

(resp. if
h(q)(0)=-1
).
Then
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(n)
and so for
l^{n}
level models it holds that
|g_{\{-3,-2\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
and

|g_{\{-3,-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

g_{\{-3,-2,-1\}}
:

in this case, we know that there is at least
one state
q_{1}\in O
and at least one state
q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}\setminus S
.
Therefore, there will be at least two odd positions not taken in
h
, so
we can infer that
h\colon\{-3,\ldots 2n-5\}
.
We create
h^{\prime}
by incrementing all ranks in
h
by
four
; in this
way, we obtain a tight ranking
h^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\{0,\ldots,2n-1\}
, so
for
l^{n}
level models it holds that
|g_{\{-3,-2,-1\}}|\in\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.

Since the size of all groups is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
, for
a fixed
i
, the total number of these encodings is still

\mathcal{O}(k^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))
.
When we sum the encodings for all possible
i
’s, we obtain that the number is
bounded by
\mathcal{O}(l^{n}\cdot\mathit{tight}(n+1))
, since
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\mathit{tight}(n))=\mathcal{O}(\mathit{tight}(n+1))
.
∎

Theorem 0.D.1

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a generalized Rabin automaton with
k
generalized Rabin pairs and each
pair has at most
\ell

Inf
s. Then, the
complemented GBA has
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{kn}\mathit{tight}(n+1)^{k})
states.

Proof

Proof follows directly from
Lemma

18
. In order to complement a generalized Rabin automaton with
k
generalized Rabin pairs with at most
\ell

Inf
s, we construct a complementary automaton for each generalized Rabin pair and then we make a product of these automata and obtain a GBA accepting the complement of the original generalized Rabin automaton.
∎
